is unlikely to be reliable unless the sample size is large. Notably, Algorithm
1
demonstrates the highest power, which is not surprising since it is specifically designed to detect incompatible correlation matrices. As expected, the test based on
d^{2}_{\mu}
shows no power, as it is only sensitive to inconsistencies in the means, which are consistent in this particular scenario. For higher dimensions (
d\geq 4
), Little’s test cannot be applied, while our test remains valid since it has no constraints on
\mathbb{S}
. In Figure
4
, we show the power function of our bootstrap tests in the case of a
d
-cycle, with
d\in\{100,200\}
, with
\theta_{2}=\ldots=\theta_{d}=\frac{\pi}{2(d-1)}
, and varying
\theta_{1}
in
[\pi/2,5\pi/8]
. We repeat the procedure
H=100
times, and report the average decision as an estimate of the power function.

Figure 4:
Simulation of the power functions of our method with
B=99
(blue) for
d=100
(left), and
d=200
(right), with Gaussian data. In each example, we fix
\theta_{2}=\ldots=\theta_{d}=\frac{\pi}{2(d-1)}
, and vary
\theta_{1}
in
[\pi/2,5\pi/8]
. For each of this setting, we repeat the experiment
H=100
times, and report the average decision. The nominal level
\alpha=0.05
in red.

Our simulations so far have used Gaussian data, so that Little’s test is valid. We now repeat our simulations with a heavy-tailed data distribution in order to assess the robustness of the methods. To this end, we consider again a
3
-cycle, and generate
X_{\{j,j+1\},i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\log N(\boldsymbol{0}_{2},\Sigma_{\{j,j+1\}})
for all
i\in[200],j\in[3]
, where
\log N(\boldsymbol{0}_{2},\Sigma_{\{j,j+1\}})
stands for the log-normal distribution, meaning that if
Y\sim\log N(\boldsymbol{0}_{2},\Sigma_{\{j,j+1\}})
then
Y_{i}=e^{Z_{i}}
, with
Z\sim N(\boldsymbol{0}_{2},\Sigma_{\{j,j+1\}})
. Here we vary
\theta_{1}\in[\pi/2+\pi/12,\pi/12]
, with
(\theta_{2},\theta_{3})=(3\pi/4,\pi/4)
. Figure
3
shows the analogue of Figure
3
, in the case of artificial data from a multivariate log-normal distribution rather than a Gaussian distribution. Note however that the covariance matrices do not coincide with the original
\Sigma_{\{j,j+1\}}
, hence on the
x
-axis we decided to estimate
R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})
using
R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})
using an independent sample from the same log-normal distribution. As in our previous results, Little’s test based on
d^{2}_{\mu}
exhibits no power but maintains Type-I error control, even when we deviate from the Gaussian setting, which aligns with a conjecture made in
Little 1988
. In contrast, Little’s test based on
d^{2}_{\mathrm{aug}}
fails to control the Type-I error.
On the other hand, our three tests exhibit similar behavior to that in the Gaussian setting, with only a slight decrease in power.

6.2
Omnibus approach

In this subsection we compare the
omnibus
and the
combined
approaches with Little’s tests in settings which are closer to real-data applications. In this regard, we generate complete artificial data according to various distributions, and then delete entries using the R package
missMethods

(
Rockel 2020
)
. MCAR data are generated with the function
delete_MCAR
, where each entry of the data matrix is deleted independently of the others with probability
p\in(0,1)
. Deviations from the null are generated by partitioning the columns in two groups, group A where the missing values are generated, and group B which determines the missingness mechanism, with two different mechanisms being considered. First,
delete_MAR_1_to_x
sets threshold values, splits the rows into two further groups depending on whether columns in group B have values greater or smaller than the threshold, and deletes some entries in columns in group A in a such a way that the probability for a value to be missing in group A divided by the
probability for a value to be missing in group B equals 1 divided by
x
, with
x
to be specified as an input parameter. Second,
delete_MAR_rank
deletes each entry in a column of group A with probability proportional to the rank of the same row in the corresponding column of group B. For further details on these functions, and other methods to generate MCAR, MAR, MNAR data, refer to
Santos et al. 2019
. These three functions were also chosen in the numerical analysis of a test of MCAR based on U-statistics in
Aleksić 2024
. Before discussing the simulation results, we note that in these settings, under the alternative, the collection of correlation matrices is only mildly incompatible (as verified by numerical inspection), while the inconsistency in means plays a critical role in detecting departures from MCAR. Consequently, the test based on
p_{R}
demonstrates low power, unlike our
omnibus
approach and Little’s test based on
d^{2}_{\mu}
.

Figure 5:
Size for MCAR data generate with
delete_MCAR(p)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from Clayton copula with parameter
1
and
N(0,1)
margins.
B=99,H=500
.

Figure 6:
Power function for MAR data generate with
delete_MAR_1_to_x(p, x = 9)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from the same Clayton copula.
B=99,H=500
.

Figure 7:
Power function for MAR data generate with
delete_MAR_rank(p)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from the same Clayton copula.
B=99,H=500
.

Figure 8:
Size for MCAR data generate with
delete_MCAR(p)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from Clayton copula with parameter
1
and
\operatorname{Exp}(1)
margins.
B=99,H=500
.

Figure 9:
Power function for MAR data generate with
delete_MAR_1_to_x(p, x = 9)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from the same Clayton copula.
B=99,H=500
.

Figure 10:
Power function for MAR data generate with
delete_MAR_rank(p)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from the same Clayton copula.
B=99,H=500
.

Figure 11:
Size for MCAR data generate with
delete_MCAR(p)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from Clayton copula with parameter
1
and
\log N(0,1)
margins.
B=99,H=500
.

Figure 12:
Power function for MAR data generate with
delete_MAR_1_to_x(p, x = 9)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from the same Clayton copula.
B=99,H=500
.

Figure 13:
Power function for MAR data generate with
delete_MAR_rank(p)
, for varying probability
p
of having a missing value. Data from the same Clayton copula.
B=99,H=500
.

For Figures
7
,
7
,
7
, we generated
3
-dimensional datasets of sample size
n=200
distributed according to a Clayton copula, with parameter
1
and Gaussian (
N(0,1)
) margins, using the function
mvdc
from the R-package
copula

Hofert et al. 2020
. For Figure
7
we deleted the first two variables with
delete_MCAR(p)
for different values of
p\in\{0.05,\ldots,0.40\}
, in order to get an artificial setting coming from the null. For each
p
, we repeat the simulation
H=500
times, and report the average Type-I error. Alternatives to the null were generated using
delete_MAR_1_to_x
, with
x=9
, for Figure
7
, and
delete_MAR_rank
for Figure
7
. Again, for each
p
, we repeat the simulations
500
times, and report the average power. In this setting, Figures
7
and
7
demonstrate that all tests, apart from the one based on
p_{R}
, perform similarly in terms of power.
The behaviour of the test based on
p_{R}
can be attributed to the fact that, under the alternative, the collection of correlation matrices is only mildly incompatible, with numerical inspection suggesting that incompatibility decreases as
p
increases. Meanwhile, Figure
7
seems to indicate that all tests control the Type-I error at the nominal level
\alpha
, except possibly for Little’s test based on
d^{2}_{\mathrm{aug}}
. This outcome supports once more the conjecture in
Little 1988
where it is suggested that even under normality, the asymptotic null distribution of
d^{2}_{\mathrm{aug}}
is unlikely to be reliable unless the sample size is large. We then move beyond the Gaussian setting and explore different marginal distributions. Specifically, we consider exponential distributions (
\operatorname{Exp}(1)
) in Figures
10
,
10
,
10
, with
d=3
, and log-normal distributions (
\log N(0,1)
) in Figures
13
,
13
,
13
, with
d=5
.
In the setting of log-normal data, Figures
10
,
10
show a slight loss in power for our
omnibus
and
combined
approaches. However, this is offset by their better control of the Type-I error (Figure
10
) compared to Little’s test based on
d^{2}_{\mu}
, and especially
d^{2}_{\mathrm{aug}}
, which consistently fails to recognize the null and always rejects. The results for the exponential setting fall between those of the Gaussian and log-normal settings.

Overall, these simulations demonstrate the effectiveness of our procedures. The
combined
approach could serve as a valid extension of Little’s test based on
d^{2}_{\mu}
when both means and covariance matrices are taken into account, rather than just means. Interestingly, incorporating
d^{2}_{\mu}
with Algorithms
1
and
3
does not inflate the Type-I error, which is a significant limitation of Little’s test based on
d^{2}_{\mathrm{aug}}
. On the other hand, while the
omnibus
approach exhibits slightly weaker performance in terms of power, it provides more rigorous control of the Type-I error. Additionally, it offers two further advantages compared to the
combined
approach: (i) it can be applied to any missingness pattern
\mathbb{S}
, even when not all pairs of variables are observed simultaneously; and (ii) it remains effective even when the dimensionality
d
is large, as it does not depend on the EM algorithm, which is known to encounter issues in practice when
d>50
(see the documentation of the R function
na.test
in
Yanagida 2024
).

7
Proofs

7.1
Proof for Section
2

Proof of Theorem
1
.

We start by proving (i). Let
P_{\mathbb{S}}\in\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{S}}^{-C_{\alpha,2}}(0)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}

\displaystyle\left\{p_{R}\leq\alpha\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{1+\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathbbm{1}\{R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},b})\geq R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\}\leq\alpha(1+B),R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\leq 3/4\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})>3/4\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})>0,R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\leq 3/4\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})>3/4\right\}\leq 2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})>0\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2}>C_{\alpha,2}\right\}\leq 2\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}-\Sigma_{S}\|_{2}>C_{\alpha,2}\right\}\leq\alpha,

using (
7.3
) and the computations thereafter. As for part (ii), suppose
R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})>2\rho
, with
\rho\in(0,1/2)
to be chosen later, and observe that we can make a Type II error only under the event
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}:=\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\leq 3/4\}
. Then, for all
P_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}
and
B\geq 2(1-\alpha)/\alpha
, we can use Markov’s inequality to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}

\displaystyle\left\{p_{R}>\alpha\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{1+\sum_{i=1}^{B}\mathbbm{1}\{R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},b})\geq R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\}>\alpha(1+B),\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\leq\frac{B\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\geq R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}}{\alpha(B+1)-1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\geq R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})<\rho,\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\geq\rho,\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{|R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})|>\rho\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{|R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})-R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}})|\geq\rho,\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\right),

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})>2\rho
and
R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}})=0
. It is then enough to find
\rho\in(0,1/2)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{|R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})|>\rho\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{|R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})-R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}})|\geq\rho,\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\leq\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}.

(9)

To achieve this, we will draw on results from the proofs of Theorem
7
and Proposition
14
, which are deferred to later sections. In particular, the analysis of the first term in (
9
) follows directly from Theorem
7
. For the second term, we will draw on ideas from the proof of Proposition
14
to demonstrate that it suffices to control the spectral norm of the covariance matrix of the bootstrap sample, which can be analysed using Proposition
33
in Appendix
D
. Throughout the following, to assist the reader in following the argument, we will explicitly reference the equations from the proofs of Theorem
7
and Proposition
14
whenever they are used. Now, if we define the
good set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}:=

\displaystyle\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\leq 2\nu^{2},\|\widehat{D}_{\mathbb{S}}^{-1/2}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\leq 2/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}},\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}},\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad\quad 2\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|X_{S,i}-\mu_{S}\|_{2}\leq\underbrace{16\sqrt{\frac{2\nu^{2}|S|}{c\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}}}+8\sqrt{\frac{2\nu^{2}\log(12|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)}{c\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}}}}_{=:M}\right.\Bigg\},

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}

\displaystyle\left\{|R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})|>\rho\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{|R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})-R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}})|\geq\rho,\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{|R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})|>\rho\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{|R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})-R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}})|>\rho/2,\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:R_to_spectral}}{\leq}\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c\rho/2,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1}-\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c\rho/8,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}^{\complement}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:ProbSigmaHatSucceq}}{\leq}\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c\rho/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1}-\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c\rho/8,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Omega_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>\nu^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{D}_{\mathbb{S}}^{-1/2}D_{\mathbb{S}}^{1/2}-I_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>1\right\}+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c/2\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{2\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|X_{S,i}-\mu_{S}\|_{2}>M,\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c\rho/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1}-\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c\rho/8,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Omega_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>\nu^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+4\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{D}_{\mathbb{S}}^{-1/2}D_{\mathbb{S}}^{1/2}-I_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>1\right\}+4\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c/2\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|X_{S,i}-\mu_{S}\|_{2}>4\nu\sqrt{|S|}+2\nu\sqrt{\log(12|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:conc_corr_to_cov_3},\eqref{eq:conc_corr_to_cov_4}}{\leq}40\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Omega_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}c\rho/48\nu^{2}\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1}-\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>c\rho/8,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|X_{S,i}-\mu_{S}\|_{2}>4\nu\sqrt{|S|}+2\nu\sqrt{\log(12|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Prop.}\ref{prop:norm_SG}}{\leq}40\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Omega_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}c\rho/48\nu^{2}\right\}+|\mathbb{S}|\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>c\rho/8,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+\alpha\beta/6.

(10)

This shows that it is enough to choose
\rho
such that

\displaystyle\max\left(40\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Omega_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}c\rho/48\nu^{2}\right\},|\mathbb{S}|\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>c\rho/8,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\right)\leq\alpha\beta/6.

(11)

As for the first term in the maximum, an analogous argument to that employed in the proof of Theorem
7
ensures that
40\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Omega_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}>\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}c\rho/48\nu^{2}\right\}\leq\alpha\beta/6
if
\rho\geq C_{\alpha\beta,c}

for a sufficiently large absolute constant
K_{1}>0
. As for the latter term in (
11
), we focus on finding a
\rho

such that
\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>c\rho/8,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\leq\alpha\beta/6|\mathbb{S}|
. This would conclude the proof. Now, first observe that under
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}
, (
5
) implies that

\|\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{1-R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}\|_{2}\leq 4\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}\|_{2}=4\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{\Omega}_{S}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\leq 4\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}^{2}\|\widehat{\Omega}_{S}\|_{2},

which is upper bounded by
32\nu^{2}/\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}
under
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}
. Hence, using similar steps as in (
7.3
) and (
7.3
), for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
and
x\in[0,1]
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>x,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim\eqref{eq:conc_corr_to_cov}}{\leq}\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>x/2,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{D}_{S,1}^{-1/2}-I_{S}\|_{2}(1+\|\widehat{D}_{S,1}^{-1/2}\|_{2})>\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}x/64\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>x/2,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{D}_{S,1}^{-1/2}-I_{S}\|_{2}>\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}x/192\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{D}_{S,1}^{-1/2}\|_{2}>2,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>x/2,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+2\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{D}_{S,1}^{-1/2}-I_{S}\|_{2}>\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}x/192\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim\eqref{eq:conc_corr_to_cov_3}}{\leq}\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>x/2,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+4\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}x/384\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 5\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}x/384\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\},

(12)

which shows that it is enough to give a concentration bound for the sample covariance matrix of the bootstrap sample. In this regard,
recall that Algorithm
1
generates a bootstrap sample from the rotated data
\tilde{X}_{\mathbb{S}}:=(\tilde{X}_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})
, which for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
and
i\in[n_{S}]
is of the form
\tilde{X}_{S,i}=\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})
, where
\widehat{Q}_{S}
comes from the dual decomposition
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}=(1-R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}))\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}+R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\widehat{\Sigma}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{S}}
. As a result, for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
, the sample correlation of
\tilde{X}_{S}
coincides with the sample covariance, and is equal to
\widehat{Q}_{S}
. Also, observe that, since
\tilde{X}_{S,i}=\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})
, we have
\tilde{X}_{S,i}^{(1)}=\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})
, where
X_{S,i}^{(1)}
is a bootstrap sample from the original set of data
X_{S}=(X_{S,1},\ldots,X_{S,n_{S}})
. This implies that

\displaystyle\widehat{\Omega}_{S,1}

\displaystyle-\widehat{Q}_{S}=n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}\tilde{X}_{S,i}^{(1)}\tilde{X}_{S,i}^{(1)T}-\left(n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}\tilde{X}_{S,i}^{(1)}\right)\left(n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}\tilde{X}_{S,i}^{(1)}\right)^{T}-\widehat{Q}_{S}

\displaystyle=n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})^{T}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad-\left(n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\right)\left(n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\right)^{T}-\widehat{Q}_{S}

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}\left\{n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}[\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})][\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})]^{T}-I_{|S|}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad\quad\quad\quad-\left(n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\right)\left(n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\right)^{T}\right\}\widehat{Q}_{S}^{1/2}.

We thus get for all
x\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>x,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}[\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})][\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})]^{T}-I_{|S|}\|_{2}>x/2,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}^{2}\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}^{2}\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\|_{2}^{2}>x/2,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}[\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})][\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})]^{T}-I_{|S|}\|_{2}>\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}x/64\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\|_{2}^{2}>c\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}x/512\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}.

(13)

As for the first term in (
7.1
), observe that, under
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}
and conditionally on the data
X_{\mathbb{S}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\|_{2}\leq\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\|_{2}\leq\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|X_{S,i}-\widehat{\mu}_{S}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\left\{\|\widehat{\mu}_{S}-\mu_{S}\|_{2}+\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|X_{S,i}-\mu_{S}\|_{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\left\{\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}(X_{S,i}-\mu_{S})\|_{2}+\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|X_{S,i}-\mu_{S}\|_{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}\|X_{S,i}-\mu_{S}\|_{2}\leq M\quad\text{ a.s.}.

This, together with Proposition
33
in Appendix
D
implies that there exists a universal constant
K_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left\{\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}[\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})][\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})]^{T}-I_{|S|}\|_{2}>\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}x/64\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}[\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})][\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}^{-1/2}\widehat{D}_{S}^{-1/2}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})]^{T}-I_{|S|}\|_{2}>\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}x/64\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left\{-\frac{K_{2}\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}n_{S}x^{2}}{\nu^{4}M^{2}\log n_{S}}\right\}.

(14)

As for the second term in (
7.1
), calling
W=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{n_{S}})\sim\operatorname{Multinomial}(n_{S},[n_{S}],(n_{S}^{-1},\ldots,n_{S}^{-1}))
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left\{\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}(X_{S,i}^{(1)}-\widehat{\mu}_{S})\|_{2}^{2}>c\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}x/512\nu^{2},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}(W_{i}-1)(X_{S,i}-\mu_{S})\|_{2}>\sqrt{c\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}x/512\nu^{2}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}|W_{i}-1|\leq 3\log(60|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}|W_{i}-1|>3\log(60|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{S}}(W_{i}-1)(X_{S,i}-\mu_{S})\|_{2}>\sqrt{c\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}x/512\nu^{2}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}},\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}|W_{i}-1|\leq 3\log(60|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)\mid W\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}|W_{i}-1|>3\log(60|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)\right\}\overset{\text{ Prop. }\ref{prop:norm_SG}}{\leq}5^{|S|}\exp\left\{-\frac{n_{S}c\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}x}{192^{2}\nu^{4}\log^{2}(60|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)}\right\}+\alpha\beta/60|\mathbb{S}|,

(15)

where in the last inequality we used Proposition
29
in Appendix
D
, which ensures that
\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{i\in[n_{S}]}|W_{i}-1|>t\right\}\leq n_{S}e^{t}/(t+1)^{t+1}\leq n_{S}e^{-t/3}
for
t\geq 1
. Now, combining (
7.1
), (
7.1
), (
7.1
), (
7.1
) gives for all
S\in\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P_{\mathbb{S}}}

\displaystyle\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S,1}-\widehat{Q}_{S}\|_{2}>c\rho/8,\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\leq 10\exp\left\{-\frac{K_{2}\sigma^{10}_{\mathrm{min}}c^{3}n_{S}\rho^{2}}{\nu^{10}\log n_{S}\{|S|+\log(|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)\}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+5^{|S|+1}\exp\left\{-\frac{K_{3}c^{2}\sigma^{6}_{\mathrm{min}}n_{S}\rho}{\nu^{6}\log^{2}(|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)}\right\}+\alpha\beta/12|\mathbb{S}|,

for sufficiently large universal constants
K_{2},K_{3}>0
, which is upper bounded by
\alpha\beta/6|\mathbb{S}|
if

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\geq\max\left(\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{K_{2}\nu^{5}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{5}c^{3/2}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n_{S})\log(|\mathbb{S}|/\alpha\beta)\{|S|+\log(|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)\}}{n_{S}}},\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\frac{K_{3}\nu^{6}}{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{6}c^{2}}\frac{\{|S|+\log(|\mathbb{S}|/\alpha\beta)\}\log^{2}(|\mathbb{S}|n_{S}/\alpha\beta)}{n_{S}}\right).

Taking the maximum between this and
C_{\alpha\beta,c}
completes the proof in light of (
11
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
2
.

We know from (
41
) in Appendix
A
that, in general,
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}
is compatible if and only if

\sum\limits_{j\in K}\theta_{j}\leq(|K|-1)\pi+\sum\limits_{j\not\in K}\theta_{j}

for all
K\subseteq[d]
with
|K|
odd. Additionally, we can argue as in the proof of Proposition
11
(i) to show that at most one of these inequalities can be an equality when
(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d})
are bounded away from
\{0,\pi\}
, which is true by assumption. We can thus write the null as

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{S}}(0)=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\subseteq[d]\\
|K|\text{ odd }\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum\limits_{j\in K}\theta_{j}\leq(|K|-1)\pi+\sum\limits_{j\not\in K}\theta_{j}\right\},

(16)

and its boundary as
\partial\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{S}}(0)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\subseteq[d]\\
|K|\text{ odd }\end{subarray}}F_{K}
, with

\displaystyle F_{K}=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{K}\neq K\\
|\tilde{K}|\text{ odd }\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum\limits_{j\in\tilde{K}}\theta_{j}<(|\tilde{K}|-1)\pi+\sum\limits_{j\not\in\tilde{K}}\theta_{j}\right\}\cap\left\{\sum\limits_{j\in K}\theta_{j}=(|K|-1)\pi+\sum\limits_{j\not\in K}\theta_{j}\right\},

(17)

which shows that, in the case of a non-singular
d
-cycle, being on the boundary of the null hypothesis is equivalent to being in the relative interior of a face of the convex polyhedron defined in (
16
). Now, the first part of the result about the asymptotic validity in the interior of (
16
) follows from Theorem
1
, so that it is enough to analyse what happens on the boundary. In this regard, we will establish the asymptotic validity of our test for the case when
(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d})\in F_{\{1\}}
(i.e.
K=\{1\}
in (
17
)), noting that the validity for the other cases can be demonstrated analogously. In this context, since on the boundary we have
R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\leq 3/4
with high probability (w.h.p.) — a fact that follows by inverting the bound provided in Theorem
7
— it suffices to analyse the bootstrap procedure. For asymptotic validity, we need to show that when
(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d})\in F_{\{1\}}
, the random variables
R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})
and
R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}
converge in distribution to the same limiting law. We will now analyse each quantity separately.

As for the former, since
\min\{1+\cos\theta_{j},1-\cos\theta_{j}\}\geq c
for all
j\in[d]
(assumption A2), we have w.h.p.

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j\in\tilde{K}}\widehat{\theta}_{j}<(|\tilde{K}|-1)\pi+\sum\limits_{j\not\in\tilde{K}}\widehat{\theta}_{j}\text{ for all }\tilde{K}\neq\{1\}.

(18)

Under this event, an analogous argument to that in the proof of Proposition
11
(iii) gives

\displaystyle R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{2\sin\left(\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{1}+\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}{2}\right)}{1+\cos\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}\sin\left(\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}{2}\right)\right]_{+}=\left[\frac{2\sin\left(\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{1}+\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}{2}\right)}{1+\cos\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}\sin\left\{\frac{1}{2}\left(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{d}\widehat{\theta}_{j}-\widehat{\beta}\right)\right\}\right]_{+}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{2\sin\left(\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{1}+\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}{2}\right)}{1+\cos\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}\left\{\sin\left(\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{d}\widehat{\theta}_{j}}{2}\right)\cos\frac{\widehat{\beta}}{2}-\cos\left(\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{d}\widehat{\theta}_{j}}{2}\right)\sin\frac{\widehat{\beta}}{2}\right\}\right]_{+},

(19)

where
(\widehat{\varphi}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\varphi}_{d})
are as in Proposition
11
(iii), and
\widehat{\beta}\equiv\beta(\widehat{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{d})
is such that
0\leq\widehat{\beta}\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\sum_{j=2}^{d}\widehat{\theta}_{j}
and
\sum_{j=2}^{d}\widehat{\varphi}_{j}=\widehat{\beta}+\sum_{j=2}^{d}\widehat{\theta}_{j}
. Furthermore, since
R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\rightarrow}R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})=0
as
n_{\mathbb{S}}\rightarrow\infty
, we have
\widehat{\varphi}_{j}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\rightarrow}\theta_{j}
for all
j\in[d]
, which also implies that
\beta(\widehat{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\theta}_{d})\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\rightarrow}0
. This, together with a Taylor approximation of Equation (
7.1
) implies that

R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})=\frac{\sin\theta_{1}}{1+\cos\theta_{1}}\left\{(\widehat{\theta}_{1}-\theta_{1})-\sum_{j=2}^{d}(\widehat{\theta}_{j}-\theta_{j})\right\}_{+}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1/\sqrt{n}),

where
\widehat{\theta}_{j}:=\cos^{-1}(\widehat{\rho}_{j})
and
\widehat{\rho}_{j}
is Pearson’s sample correlation coefficient. Now, it is known
(
Lehmann 1999
, Example 5.4.3)
that
\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\rho}_{1}-\rho_{1})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}N(0,\gamma_{1}^{2})
, where
\gamma_{1}^{2}\equiv\gamma_{1}^{2}(P_{\{1,2\}})
was defined in (
2
). For example, if
P_{\{1,2\}}
is Gaussian, it simplifies to
\gamma_{1}^{2}=(1-\rho^{2})^{2}
. This, together with the Delta method, implies that
\sqrt{n}\{\widehat{\theta}_{j}-\theta_{j}\}\overset{d}{\rightarrow}N(0,\gamma_{j}^{2}/\sin^{2}\theta_{j})
for all
j\in[d]
, which further shows that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\frac{\sin\theta_{1}}{1+\cos\theta_{1}}N\left(0,\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\gamma_{j}^{2}}{\sin^{2}\theta_{j}}\right)_{+}

(20)

due to the independence between
\widehat{\theta}_{j_{1}}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{j_{2}}
for
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
. The limiting distribution has a point mass of
1/2
at zero, and is non-degenerate for positive values, as
\sin\theta_{1}>0
by
(\mathrm{A2})
, and there exists
j\in[d]
such that
\gamma^{2}_{j}>0
.

As for the convergence of
R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}
, we will split the proof in two steps. We will first show that
R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}
converges in distribution to (
20
), using the fact that
\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}
is bounded away from singularity and lies on
F_{\{1\}}
w.h.p., and then argue that
R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}=R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1/\sqrt{n})
. Regarding the fact that
\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}
is bounded away from singularity, observe that Proposition
11
(iii) implies that

\displaystyle\min\{1+\cos\widehat{\varphi}_{j},1-\cos\widehat{\varphi}_{j}\}\geq\min\{1+\cos\widehat{\theta}_{j},1-\cos\widehat{\theta}_{j}\}\text{ for all }j\in[d].

(21)

In order to show this, observe that, under (
18
), we have that
(1-\cos\widehat{\theta}_{1})/(1+\cos\widehat{\varphi}_{1})=1-R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\in(0,1)
, which implies that
\widehat{\varphi}_{1}\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}
. Similarly,
\widehat{\varphi}_{j}\geq\widehat{\theta}_{j}
for all
j\neq 1
. This, together with
\widehat{\varphi}_{1}=\sum_{j=2}^{d}\widehat{\varphi}_{j}
, shows that
\widehat{\theta}_{j}\leq\widehat{\varphi}_{j}\leq\widehat{\varphi}_{1}\leq\widehat{\theta}_{1}
for all
j\neq 1
, which completes the proof showing that
\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}
is at most as singular as
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}
w.h.p.. Note that this happens with high probability because the
good
event (
18
) happens with high probability. Furthermore, together with
\widehat{\varphi}_{1}=\sum_{j=2}^{d}\widehat{\varphi}_{j}
, the non-singularity of
\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}
shows that
(\widehat{\varphi}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\varphi}_{d})\in F_{\{1\}}
w.h.p..
Hence, the previous argument applies and gives

R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}=\frac{\sin\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}{1+\cos\widehat{\varphi}_{1}}\left\{(\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{(1)}-\widehat{\varphi}_{1})-\sum_{j=2}^{d}(\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{(1)}-\widehat{\varphi}_{j})\right\}_{+}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1/\sqrt{n}),

where
\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{(1)}:=\cos^{-1}(\widehat{\rho}_{j}^{(1)})
and
\widehat{\rho}_{j}^{(1)}
is the Pearson’s sample correlation coefficient for the bootstrap sample, and
\widehat{\varphi}_{1}
is such that
\widehat{\varphi}_{1}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\rightarrow}\theta_{1}
. Now, observe that
\sqrt{n}\{\widehat{\rho}^{(1)}_{1}-\cos\widehat{\varphi}_{1}\}\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}\overset{d}{\rightarrow}N(0,\gamma_{1}^{2})
. To see why this is the case, calling
\tilde{P}_{n_{\{1,2\}}}^{\{1,2\}}:=n^{-1}_{\{1,2\}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\{1,2\}}}\tilde{X}_{\{1,2\},i}
with
\tilde{X}_{\{1,2\},i}=\widehat{Q}^{1/2}_{\{1,2\}}\widehat{\Sigma}^{-1/2}_{\{1,2\}}\operatorname{diag}^{-1/2}(\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{\{1,2\}})(X_{\{1,2\},i}-\widehat{\mu}_{\{1,2\}})
, we have that
\gamma^{2}(\tilde{P}_{n_{\{1,2\}}}^{\{1,2\}})
converges in probability to the
\gamma^{2}(\cdot)
coefficient of the standardised distribution
\operatorname{diag}^{-1/2}(\sigma^{2}_{\{1,2\}})(X_{\{1,2\},1}-\mu_{\{1,2\}})
, which is equal to
\gamma_{1}^{2}
as
\gamma^{2}(\cdot)
is invariant under standardisation (to see why, recall the definitions of
\gamma^{2}(P)
,
f(u,v,w)
and
S
in (
2
)). We can then reproduce the same argument as before, replacing
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}
with
\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}
, and conclude using Slutsky’s theorem that
R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}
converges in distribution to the limiting normal distribution described in (
20
).

As for the second step, observe that, since
P_{\{j,j+1\}}
has finite fourth moments for all
j\in[d]
, we have that
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1}\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\rightarrow}\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}}
, hence
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}\geq\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}})/2
w.h.p.. On the other hand, we also proved in (
21
) that
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\widehat{Q}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})=\widehat{c}
w.h.p., hence
R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}=R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1/\sqrt{n})
using the definition of
R_{z}(\cdot)
in (
7
).

Putting all the pieces together, we have just shown that also
\sqrt{n}R_{\widehat{c}/2}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S},1})\mid X_{\mathbb{S}}
converges in distribution to (
20
). Now, calling
r_{n_{\mathbb{S}}}
the critical value of the bootstrap test when
B\rightarrow\infty
for fixed
n_{\mathbb{S}}
, this shows that
r_{n_{\mathbb{S}}}
converges to the
(1-\alpha)
-quantile of the distribution in (
20
), which is positive for
\alpha<1/2
. Since the limiting distribution of
\sqrt{n}R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})
is the same, we conclude that the probability of rejecting the null hypothesis on the boundary converges to
\alpha
.
∎

7.2
Proofs for Section
3

Proof of Proposition
3
.

For any
X\in\mathcal{M}
and
X_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{S}}
we have

\displaystyle\langle AX,X_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}((AX)_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}(X_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}=\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}X_{jj^{\prime}}(X_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{d}X_{jj^{\prime}}\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbbm{1}_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}(X_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}=\langle X,A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle,

as claimed.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4
.

The strategy is to use a semi-definite programming version of Farkas’ lemma. This is well known in the relevant literature, but we provide a statement and short proof for completeness; see Proposition
26
in Appendix
C
. First, rewrite the matrix completion problem

\text{find }\Sigma\in\mathcal{M}\text{ such that }\begin{cases}\Sigma_{jj^{\prime}}=(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}},\forall S\in\mathbb{S}_{jj^{\prime}}\\
\Sigma\succcurlyeq 0\end{cases}

as

\displaystyle\text{find }\Sigma\in\mathcal{M}\text{ such that }\begin{cases}\langle\Sigma,E_{jj^{\prime}}\rangle=(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}},\forall S\in\mathbb{S}_{jj^{\prime}}\\
\Sigma\succcurlyeq 0\end{cases}

(22)

where
E_{jj^{\prime}}=(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j^{\prime}}^{T}+\boldsymbol{e}_{j^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{T})/2
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
is the
j
-th column vector of the standard orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In order to apply the semi-definite version of Farkas’ lemma we transform our problem so that the equality constraints have zero on the right-hand side. To this end, define

H_{j}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{T}/2\\
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}/2&\boldsymbol{O}\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }G_{S,jj^{\prime}}:=\begin{pmatrix}-(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}&\boldsymbol{0}^{T}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&E_{jj^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix},

and consider the completion problem

\displaystyle\text{find }\tilde{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{M}\text{ such that }\begin{cases}\langle\tilde{\Sigma},H_{j}\rangle=0,\forall j\in[d]\\
\langle\tilde{\Sigma},G_{S,jj^{\prime}}\rangle=0,\forall S\in\mathbb{S}_{jj^{\prime}}\\
\tilde{\Sigma}\succcurlyeq 0.\end{cases}

(23)

The condition
\langle\tilde{\Sigma},H_{j}\rangle=0,\forall j\in[d]
forces
\tilde{\Sigma}
to be in block diagonal form

\tilde{\Sigma}:=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{0,0}&\boldsymbol{0}^{T}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\Sigma\end{pmatrix}.

Now, observe that (
22
) has a solution if and only if (
23
) has a non-zero solution. Indeed, for every solution
\Sigma_{0}
of (
22
), then
\operatorname{diag}(1,\Sigma_{0})
is a solution of (
23
). On the other hand, suppose that
\tilde{\Sigma}_{0}=\operatorname{diag}(\gamma_{0,0},\Sigma_{0})\neq\boldsymbol{O}
is a solution of (
23
). This implies that
\gamma_{0,0}\neq 0
, otherwise
0=\langle\tilde{\Sigma}_{0},G_{S,jj^{\prime}}\rangle=-\gamma_{0,0}(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}+\Sigma_{jj^{\prime}}=\Sigma_{jj^{\prime}}
, which would imply
\tilde{\Sigma}_{0}=\boldsymbol{O}
. Being
\gamma_{0,0}\neq 0
, we can rescale the bigger block in
\tilde{\Sigma}_{0}
by
\gamma_{0,0}
, i.e.
\Sigma_{0}=:\gamma_{0,0}G
, and get
0=\langle\tilde{\Sigma}_{0},G_{S,jj^{\prime}}\rangle=-\gamma_{0,0}(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}+\gamma_{0,0}G_{jj^{\prime}}=-(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}+G_{jj^{\prime}}
, which shows that
G
is a solution of (
22
). This further implies that we can assume without loss of generality that
\gamma_{0,0}=1
when (
23
) admits a non-zero solution. Now, by Proposition
26
, we know that (
23
) has a non-zero solution
\tilde{\Sigma}=\operatorname{diag}(1,\Sigma)
if and only if

\sum\limits_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum\limits_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}(X_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}G_{S,jj^{\prime}}=\sum\limits_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\begin{pmatrix}-\langle\Sigma_{S},X_{S}\rangle&\boldsymbol{0}^{T}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\frac{1}{2}X_{S}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\langle\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}},X_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle&\boldsymbol{0}^{T}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\frac{1}{2}A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}\end{pmatrix}\nsucc 0,

for all collections of matrices
X_{\mathbb{S}}
, not necessarily PSD. Now, this block matrix is positive definite if and only if both
A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}\succ 0
and
\langle\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}},X_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle<0
. Hence, (
23
) has a non-zero solution if and only if
\langle\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}},X_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle\geq 0
for all
X_{\mathbb{S}}
such that
A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}\succ 0
, and the claim follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5
.

Weak duality, i.e. LHS
\leq
RHS, always holds for SDPs (see
Blekherman et al. 2012
), but we include a short proof for the sake of completeness. In fact, for any
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}
, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\inf\{\epsilon\in[0,1]:\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\in(1-\epsilon)\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}+\epsilon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}\}

(24)

as

\displaystyle\inf\{\epsilon\in[0,1]:\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\in(1-\epsilon)\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}+\epsilon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}\}

\displaystyle=1-\sup\{\epsilon\in[0,1]:\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\in\epsilon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}+(1-\epsilon)\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}\}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{d}\sup\{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma):\Sigma\in\mathcal{P}^{*},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-A\Sigma\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0,\Sigma_{11}=\ldots=\Sigma_{dd}\}.

Now, for any
Y_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}^{*}
such that
A^{*}Y_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq I_{d}\text{ for some }Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, and any
\Sigma\in\mathcal{P}^{*}
such that
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-A\Sigma\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle=\langle I_{d},\Sigma\rangle=-\langle A^{*}Y_{\mathbb{S}}+Y-I_{d},\Sigma\rangle+\langle A^{*}Y_{\mathbb{S}}+Y,\Sigma\rangle\leq\langle A^{*}Y_{\mathbb{S}},\Sigma\rangle+\langle Y,\Sigma\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle A^{*}Y_{\mathbb{S}},\Sigma\rangle=\langle Y_{\mathbb{S}},A\Sigma\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}=\langle Y_{\mathbb{S}},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}-\langle Y_{\mathbb{S}},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-A\Sigma\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}\leq\langle Y_{\mathbb{S}},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}.

This shows that (
24
)
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{d}\inf\{\langle Y_{\mathbb{S}},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}:Y_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}^{*},A^{*}Y_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq I_{d}\}.

(25)

Weak duality follows upon noting that
A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}=I_{d}
and
\langle X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}=d
and setting
X_{\mathbb{S}}=Y_{\mathbb{S}}-X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}
. This is not surprising, as we already mentioned that weak duality always holds for SDP problems.

We will now prove strong duality for this problem. Our strategy is to write our primal and dual problems in standard form and check Slater’s condition for the primal problem (
25
). We already mentioned that (
24
) can be written as

1-\frac{1}{d}\sup\{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma):\Sigma\in\mathcal{P}^{*},\Sigma_{11}=\ldots=\Sigma_{dd},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-A\Sigma\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0\}.

We now write this maximisation problem in standard form by introducing variables
(Z_{S}:S\in\mathbb{S})=\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-A\Sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}^{*}
. Enumerating
\mathbb{S}
as
\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}\}
, we instead optimise over block-diagonal matrices of the form

X=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma&0&\cdots&0\\
0&Z_{S_{1}}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&Z_{S_{m}}\end{pmatrix}

For such
X
our constraints are equivalent to
X\succeq 0
,

\langle E_{jj}-E_{11},X\rangle=0\quad\text{ for }j=2,\ldots,d

and

\langle E_{jj^{\prime}}+E_{S,jj^{\prime}},X\rangle=(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}\quad\text{ for }S\in\mathbb{S}\text{ and }j,j^{\prime}\in S,

where
E_{jj^{\prime}}=(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j^{\prime}}^{T}+\boldsymbol{e}_{j^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{T})/2
is the binary symmetric matrix of the same dimension as
X
with its only non-zero entries being in the
(j,j^{\prime})
-th and
(j^{\prime},j)
-th positions of the top left block, and where
E_{S,jj^{\prime}}=(\boldsymbol{e}_{S,j}\boldsymbol{e}_{S,j^{\prime}}^{T}+\boldsymbol{e}_{S,j^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{e}_{S,j}^{T})/2
is the binary symmetric matrix of the same dimension as
X
with its only non-zero entries being in the
(j,j^{\prime})
-th and
(j^{\prime},j)
-th positions of the block occupied by
Z_{S}
in
X
. Write
C
for the diagonal matrix of the same dimension as
X
with
I_{d}
in the top left block, and all other entries equal to zero. It is now possible to write

\displaystyle\sup

\displaystyle\{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma):\Sigma\in\mathcal{P}^{*},\quad\Sigma_{11}=\ldots=\Sigma_{dd},\quad\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-A\Sigma\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0\}

\displaystyle=\sup\{\langle C,X\rangle:X\succeq 0\text{ is block diagonal},\langle E_{jj}-E_{11},X\rangle=0\text{ for }j=2,\ldots,d,

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\langle E_{jj^{\prime}}+E_{S,jj^{\prime}},X\rangle=(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}\quad\text{ for }S\in\mathbb{S}\text{ and }j,j^{\prime}\in S\},

(26)

so that our dual problem (
24
) is now in standard form. Our primal problem (
25
) is put into standard form by writing

\displaystyle\inf\{

\displaystyle\langle\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}},Y_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle:A^{*}Y_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq I_{d},\quad Y_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0,\quad Y_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{S}},\quad Y\in\mathcal{Y}\}

\displaystyle=\inf\biggl\{\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}y_{S,jj^{\prime}}:\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}y_{S,jj^{\prime}}E_{jj^{\prime}}+\sum_{j=2}^{d}y_{jj}(E_{jj}-E_{11})\succeq I_{d},

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}y_{S,jj^{\prime}}E_{S,jj^{\prime}}\succeq 0\text{ for all }S\in\mathbb{S},\quad y_{jj},y_{S,jj^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ for all }S,j,j^{\prime}\biggr\}.

(27)

With the problems written in standard form, it is now clear that (
7.2
) is the dual problem associated to (
7.2
); see Theorem 3.1 in
Vandenberghe and Boyd 1996
. Observe further that the primal problem is strictly feasible since
Y_{\mathbb{S}}=X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}
satisfies the linear constraints with
Y
equal to the zero matrix. Hence, by standard duality results (Theorem 2.15 in
Blekherman et al. 2012
, Theorem 3.1 in
Vandenberghe and Boyd 1996
), we have that

\displaystyle\sup\{\langle C,X\rangle:X\succeq 0\text{ is block diagonal},\langle E_{jj}-E_{11},X\rangle=0\text{ for }j=2,\ldots,d,

\displaystyle\hskip 200.0pt\langle E_{jj^{\prime}}+E_{S,jj^{\prime}},X\rangle=(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}\quad\text{ for }S\in\mathbb{S}\text{ and }j,j^{\prime}\in S\}

\displaystyle=\inf\biggl\{\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}(\Sigma_{S})_{jj^{\prime}}y_{S,jj^{\prime}}:\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}y_{S,jj^{\prime}}E_{jj^{\prime}}+\sum_{j=2}^{d}y_{jj}(E_{jj}-E_{11})\succeq I_{d},

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in S}y_{S,jj^{\prime}}E_{S,jj^{\prime}}\succeq 0\text{ for all }S\in\mathbb{S},\quad y_{jj},y_{S,jj^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ for all }S,j,j^{\prime}\biggr\},

and the result follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6
.

(i) Convexity follows easily from basic properties of the supremum. Indeed, consider
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}:=\lambda\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{(1)}+(1-\lambda)\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{(2)}
with
\lambda\in[0,1]
. Observe that
R
is well defined at
\tilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}
, as the convex combination of correlation matrices is still a correlation matrix. Then, for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle R(\tilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})

\displaystyle=\sup\biggr\{-\frac{1}{d}\langle X_{\mathbb{S}},\tilde{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle:X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0,A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq 0\text{ for some }Y\in\mathcal{Y}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\sup\biggr\{-\frac{1}{d}\langle X_{\mathbb{S}},\lambda\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{(1)}+(1-\lambda)\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{(2)}\rangle:X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0,A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq 0\text{ for some }Y\in\mathcal{Y}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\sup\biggr\{-\frac{1}{d}\langle X_{\mathbb{S}},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{(1)}\rangle:X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0,A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq 0\text{ for some }Y\in\mathcal{Y}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+(1-\lambda)\sup\biggr\{-\frac{1}{d}\langle X_{\mathbb{S}},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{(2)}\rangle:X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0,A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq 0\text{ for some }Y\in\mathcal{Y}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\lambda R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{(1)})+(1-\lambda)R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{(2)}),

and the convexity of
R(\cdot)
follows.

(ii)
R
acts on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}
, which is the space of correlation matrices over the patterns
\mathbb{S}
. Now, the spectrahedron of all correlation matrices of dimension
p
,

\mathcal{E}_{p}=\left\{\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\binom{p}{2}}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{p}{2}}:\Sigma_{X}=\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{1}&\cdots&x_{p-1}\\
x_{1}&1&\cdots&x_{2p-3}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{p-1}&x_{2p-3}&\cdots&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0\right\},

is called the
elliptope
, and identifies a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{\binom{p}{2}}
. This follows from the fact that the symmetry condition
\Sigma_{X}=\Sigma_{X}^{T}
defines a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
of dimension
\binom{p}{2}
, while the PSD condition
v^{T}\Sigma_{X}v\geq 0
for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
defines a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{\binom{p}{2}}
, which is a convex cone. For further insights, refer to
Laurent and Poljak 1996
. This implies that, for every pattern
\mathbb{S}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{S}}
can be identified with a closed subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{s}
, where
s=\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\binom{|S|}{2}
. The continuity of
R
follows from the fact that every convex function that is finite on
\mathbb{R}^{s}
is necessarily continuous (see Corollary 10.1.1. in
Rockafellar 1970
).

To prove (iii), we will make use of the fact that the dual characterisation allows us to express
R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}})
as

1-\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}\sup\{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma):\Sigma\in\mathcal{P}^{*},\Sigma_{11}=\ldots=\Sigma_{d^{\prime}d^{\prime}},\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}-A_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}\Sigma\succeq_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}0\},

where
d^{\prime}=|\cup_{S\in\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}S|
. Now, let
\tilde{\Sigma}
be an optimal feasible matrix for
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
, where all the diagonal elements of
\tilde{\Sigma}
are the equal to each other by definition of
R
. Then, if we consider the restriction of
\tilde{\Sigma}
on
\cup_{S\in\mathbb{S}}S
, call it
\tilde{\Sigma}_{|\mathbb{S}}
, it is clear that
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-A_{\mathbb{S}}\tilde{\Sigma}_{|\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0
, since
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}-A_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}\Sigma\succeq_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}0
and
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
by hypothesis, while
\tilde{\Sigma}_{|\mathbb{S}}\succeq 0
follows again by Cauchy’s interlacing theorem. Hence, calling
d=|\cup_{S\in\mathbb{S}}S|
, for every
\tilde{\Sigma}
that is optimal for
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
, we can construct a feasible
\tilde{\Sigma}_{|\mathbb{S}}
for
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}
such that
1-\operatorname{tr}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{|\mathbb{S}})/d=R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}})
. This completes the proof.
∎

7.3
Proofs for Section
4

Proof of Theorem
7
.

We are interested in finding
C_{\alpha}\in(0,1)
such that
\forall\alpha\in(0,1)

\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha}\right\}\leq\alpha.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha},\,\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+1-\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}.

(28)

Since
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}cI_{\mathbb{S}}
by assumption, we may bound the second part of (
28
) by writing

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}cI_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}=\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}+\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}cI_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\geq\frac{c}{2}\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}.

This implies that

\displaystyle 1-\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\geq\frac{c}{2}\right\}.

(29)

Now, define

\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(X_{\mathbb{S}})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\mathbb{S}_{j}|^{-1}\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{j}}(X_{S})_{jj}

and observe that, for all
X_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{S}}
, we have
\langle X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0},X_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}=\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(X_{\mathbb{S}})
. See Proposition
22
in Appendix
B
for a proof of this fact. Using the arguments leading up to (
6
) above, the first term on the right-hand side of (
28
) can be written as

\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha},\,\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}=\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\sup\limits_{X_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{F}_{c/2}}-\frac{1}{d}\langle X_{\mathbb{S}},\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}\geq C_{\alpha},\,\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\},

where
\mathcal{F}_{c/2}=\{X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0,A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq 0\text{ for some }Y\in\mathcal{Y},\langle X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0},\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}\leq d\}
. Discarding the condition
A^{*}X_{\mathbb{S}}+Y\succeq 0\text{ for some }Y\in\mathcal{Y}
and enlarging our feasible to
\mathcal{\tilde{F}}_{c/2}:=\{X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}0,\langle X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0},\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}\leq d\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha},\,\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\widehat{R}-R\geq C_{\alpha},\,\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{|\widehat{R}-R|\geq C_{\alpha},\,\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\succeq_{\mathbb{S}}\frac{c}{2}I_{\mathbb{S}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\sup\limits_{X_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{\tilde{F}}_{c/2}}\left|-\frac{1}{d}\langle X_{\mathbb{S}},\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}\right|\geq C_{\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\sup\limits_{X_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{\tilde{F}}_{c/2}}\left|\langle X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0},\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\rangle_{\mathbb{S}}-(\operatorname{\bar{tr}}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-d)\right|\geq d\cdot C_{\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\cdot\sup\limits_{X_{\mathbb{S}}\in\mathcal{\tilde{F}}_{c/2}}\|X_{\mathbb{S}}+X_{\mathbb{S}}^{0}\|_{*,\mathbb{S}}\geq d\cdot C_{\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\cdot 2d/c\geq d\cdot C_{\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\right\},

(30)

where we used Holder’s inequality for collections of matrices, and the fact that
\operatorname{\bar{tr}}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})=d
, since
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}
is a collection of sample correlation matrices. Putting all the pieces together, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\geq c/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\right\},

(31)

since
\mathbb{P}\{X\geq x_{1}\}+\mathbb{P}\{X\geq x_{2}\}\leq 2\mathbb{P}\{X\geq\min\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\}
. Hence, in order to bound this probability above by
\alpha
, it is sufficient to find
C_{\alpha}
such that

\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\right\}\leq\alpha/2.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\Bigl\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}

\displaystyle\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\Bigr\}\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}-\Sigma_{S}\|_{2}\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}-\Sigma_{S}\|_{2}\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\right\}\leq|\mathbb{S}|\cdot\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}-\Sigma_{S}\|_{2}\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\right\}.

(32)

Hence, calling

\displaystyle C_{t}(S)

\displaystyle:=\frac{K_{1}\nu^{4}}{\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}}\sqrt{\frac{|S|+\log(1/t)}{n}}

for all
S\in\mathbb{S}
, with
K_{1}>0
sufficiently large universal constants, it is immediate to see using Proposition
14
that it is sufficient to take

C_{\alpha}\geq\frac{2}{c}\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}C_{\alpha/2|\mathbb{S}|}(S),

while ensuring
C_{\alpha}\leq 1
, in order to have
\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}-\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\|_{2,\mathbb{S}}\geq c\cdot C_{\alpha}/2\right\}\leq\alpha/2
, and the first statement follows. As for the last statement, observe that if
R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})>C_{\alpha}+C_{\beta}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}\}\leq C_{\alpha}\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}\leq C_{\alpha}-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})\}\leq\mathbb{P}\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})\leq-C_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\{|R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})|\geq C_{\beta}\}\leq\beta,

using the exact same concentration bound we employed to control the Type-I error.
∎

The analysis of
R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})
in Theorem
7
is crucially based on the fact that we can control the oscillation
|R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})-R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})|
using
\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}-\Sigma_{S}\|_{2}
, where the
\Sigma_{S}
are the Pearson population correlation matrices and
\widehat{\Sigma}_{S}
are the corresponding Pearson sample correlation matrices. We now state and prove a tail bound for the spectral norm
\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{2}
, where
\Sigma
is the population correlation matrix and
\widehat{\Sigma}
is the sample correlation matrix of complete data.

Proposition 14
.

Suppose we observe an i.i.d sample
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\sim X
, where
X
is
\nu
-subgaussian random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with mean
\mu
. Let
\widehat{\mu}
be the sample mean, and let
\Omega
and
\widehat{\Omega}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{T}-\widehat{\mu}\widehat{\mu}^{T}
be the population and sample covariance matrices, respectively. Let
\Sigma=D^{-1/2}\Omega D^{-1/2}
be the population correlation matrix, where
D=\operatorname{diag}(\Omega)
, and
\widehat{\Sigma}=\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{D}^{-1/2}
be the sample correlation matrix, where
\widehat{D}=\operatorname{diag}(\widehat{\Omega})
. Then, there exist a universal constant
K_{1}>0
such that, for every
t\in[0,1]
such that
C_{t}\leq 1
, we have
\mathbb{P}\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{2}>C_{t}\}\leq t
where

\displaystyle C_{t}:=\frac{K_{1}\nu^{4}}{\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/t)}{n}}

and
\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}:=\min\limits_{j\in[d]}\Omega_{jj}
.

Proof of Proposition
14
.

We will prove the claim by showing that it is sufficient to bound the spectral norm of the difference between the sample covariance matrix and its population counterpart, for which classical tail bounds apply. Now, observe that, for all
x\in[0,1]
, the triangle inequality and the sub-multiplicativity of the spectral norm imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{2}>x\right\}=\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2}\Omega D^{-1/2}\|_{2}>x\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\widehat{\Omega}\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2}\widehat{\Omega}D^{-1/2}\|_{2}>x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\|D^{-1/2}(\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega)D^{-1/2}\|_{2}>x/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\widehat{\Omega}(\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2})\|_{2}+\|(\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2})\widehat{\Omega}D^{-1/2}\|_{2}>x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}x/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{\Omega}\|_{2}(\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\|_{2}+\|D^{-1/2}\|_{2})>x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}x/2\right\}.

(33)

As for the first term in (
7.3
), using the fact that
\|D^{-1/2}\|_{2}\leq 1/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}
and
\|\Omega\|_{2}\leq\nu^{2}
due to subgaussianity, we further have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{\Omega}\|_{2}(\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\|_{2}+\|D^{-1/2}\|_{2})>x/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2}\|_{2}\|\widehat{\Omega}\|_{2}(\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\|_{2}+\|D^{-1/2}\|_{2})>x/2,\|\widehat{\Omega}\|_{2}\leq 2\nu^{2},\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\|_{2}\leq 2/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbb{P}\{\|\widehat{\Omega}\|_{2}>2\nu^{2}\}+\mathbb{P}\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}\|_{2}>2/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2}\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}x/12\nu^{2}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\nu^{2}\}+\mathbb{P}\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}-D^{-1/2}\|_{2}>1/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}D^{1/2}-I\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}x/12\nu^{2}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\nu^{2}\}+\mathbb{P}\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}D^{1/2}-I\|_{2}>1\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}D^{1/2}-I\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}x/12\nu^{2}\right\}+\mathbb{P}\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\nu^{2}\},

(34)

where in the last step we used the fact that
x\in[0,1]
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}\leq\|\Omega\|_{2}\leq\nu^{2}
. As for the first term in (
7.3
), for all
x>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left\{\|\widehat{D}^{-1/2}D^{1/2}-I\|_{2}>x\right\}=\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\sigma_{j}/\widehat{\sigma}_{j}-1|>x\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\sigma_{j}/\widehat{\sigma}_{j}-1|>x,\max_{j\in[d]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}/\sigma_{j}^{2}-1|\leq 3/4\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}/\sigma_{j}^{2}-1|>3/4\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}/\sigma_{j}-1|>x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}/\sigma_{j}^{2}-1|>3/4\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}/\sigma_{j}^{2}-1|>x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}/\sigma_{j}^{2}-1|>3/4\right\}\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}/\sigma_{j}^{2}-1|>x/2\wedge 3/4\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}|\widehat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}-\sigma_{j}^{2}|>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}(x/2\wedge 3/4)\right\}=2\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{D}-D\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}(x/2\wedge 3/4)\right\}

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\operatorname{diag}(\operatorname{diag}(\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega))\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}(x/2\wedge 3/4)\right\}\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}(x/2\wedge 3/4)\right\},

(35)

where in the second and third inequalities we used the fact that
|1/\sqrt{x}-1|\leq 2|\sqrt{x}-1|
for
|x-1|\leq 3/4
and the fact that
|\sqrt{x}-1|\leq|x-1|
, respectively. Combining (
7.3
), (
7.3
), (
7.3
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{2}>x\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\nu^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle+4\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}(3/4\wedge\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}x/24\nu^{2})\right\}\leq 6\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{4}x/24\nu^{2}\right\},

(36)

using again the fact that
x\in[0,1]
and
\sigma^{2}_{\mathrm{min}}\leq\nu^{2}
, which shows that it is enough to control
\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}
. In this regard, for all
x>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left\{\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}>x\right\}=\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{T}-\widehat{\mu}\widehat{\mu}^{T}-\Omega\|_{2}>x\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\mu)(X_{i}-\mu)^{T}-\Omega-(\widehat{\mu}-\mu)(\widehat{\mu}-\mu)^{T}\|_{2}>x\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\mu)(X_{i}-\mu)^{T}-\Omega\|_{2}>x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\widehat{\mu}-\mu\|_{2}^{2}>x/2\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\mu)(X_{i}-\mu)^{T}-\Omega\|_{2}>x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\mu)\|_{2}>\sqrt{x/2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot 9^{d}\exp\left\{-n\frac{x}{32\nu^{2}}\wedge\left(\frac{x}{32\nu^{2}}\right)^{2}\right\}+5^{d}\exp\left\{-\frac{nx}{16\nu^{2}}\right\},

where we used Propositions
28
and
32
in Appendix
D
in the last inequality. Inverting this bound leads to

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\Omega}-\Omega\|_{2}\leq K_{1}\nu^{2}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/t)}{n}}\vee\frac{d+\log(1/t)}{n},

(37)

with probability
\geq 1-t
, for a universal constant
K_{1}>0
sufficiently large. Now, combining this with (
7.3
) and (
37
) shows that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{2}\leq\frac{K_{1}\nu^{4}}{\sigma^{4}_{\mathrm{min}}}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/t)}{n}}\vee\frac{d+\log(1/t)}{n},

(38)

with probability
\geq 1-t
, and the assumption that
C_{t}\leq 1
allows focusing on the subgaussian regime. This completes the proof.
∎

First, observe that the dependence on
1/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}
is reasonable, as the smaller the minimum variance the more problematic the normalisation matrix
D^{-1/2}
. Second, observe that since we are restricting to the case
C_{t}\leq 1
, i.e.
n\gtrsim d
, the subgaussian regime prevails, and we obtain that

\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}

in probability. Similar rates, with logarithmic factors, were found in high-dimensional covariance matrix estimation with missing observations
(
Lounici 2014
)
, sample covariance matrix estimator of reduced effective rank population matrices
(
Bunea and Xiao 2015
)
, concentration of the adjacency matrix and of the Laplacian in random graphs
(
Oliveira 2010
)
, and in the statistical analysis of latent generalized correlation matrix estimation in transelliptical distribution
(
Han and Liu 2017
)
. In particular, using the additional assumption that the data is generated according to a transelliptical distribution,
Han and Liu 2017
gave an estimator
\widehat{K}
based on Kendall’s tau and proved that

\|\widehat{K}-\Sigma\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{r(\Sigma)\log(d)}{n}},

where
r(\Sigma):=\operatorname{tr}(\Sigma)/\|\Sigma\|_{2}
is the effective dimension of
\Sigma
. This is analogous to the bound given in Proposition
14
, where we have an extra factor of
\nu^{2}/\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}^{2}
, which can be interpreted as the condition number and might lead to a suboptimal bound when it is large, and the ambient dimension
d
in place of the intrinsic dimension
r(\Sigma)
. This would improve the bound sensibly in the case of an approximately low-rank correlation matrix.

7.4
Proofs for Section
5.1

Proof of Proposition
8
.

Let
\sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{2}
be a nonnegative collection such that
\bar{\operatorname{av}}_{j}(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbb{S}})=1\text{ for all }j\in[d]
. Using, for the third equality, the facts that
A_{V}\boldsymbol{1}_{d}
also satisfies these properties and that
\bar{\text{av}}
is linear, we have that

\displaystyle 1-V(\sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})

\displaystyle=1-\inf\left\{\epsilon\in[0,1]:\sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{2}=(1-\epsilon)A_{V}\boldsymbol{1}_{d}+\epsilon{\sigma^{\prime}}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2}\text{ with }\bar{\operatorname{av}}_{j}(\sigma^{\prime 2}_{\mathbb{S}})=1\text{ for all }j\in[d]\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\epsilon\in[0,1]:\sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{2}=\epsilon A_{V}\boldsymbol{1}_{d}+(1-\epsilon){\sigma^{\prime}}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2}\text{ with }\bar{\operatorname{av}}_{j}(\sigma^{\prime 2}_{\mathbb{S}})=1\text{ for all }j\in[d]\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\epsilon\in[0,1]:\epsilon\leq\min_{j\in[d]}\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{j}}\sigma_{S,j}^{2}\right\}=\min_{j\in[d]}\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{j}}\sigma_{S,j}^{2},

as claimed.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9
.

We are interested in finding
C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}\in(0,2)
such that
\forall\alpha\in(0,1)
we have

\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})+V(\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}\}\leq\alpha.

First, observe that

\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\}+\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\{V(\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\},

so that it is enough to find
C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}
such that

\max\left\{\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\},\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\{V(\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\}\right\}\leq\alpha/2.

As for the former, Theorem
7
applies, showing that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\{R(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\mathbb{S}})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\}\leq\alpha/2

(39)

if
C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}\geq 2C_{\alpha/2}
, as long as
C_{\alpha/2}\leq 1
. As for the second term, since
V(\sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})=0
under the null,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{V(\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{V(\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})-V(\sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{|V(\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})-V(\sigma_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})|\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{|\min_{j\in[d]}\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{j}}\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{S,j}-\min_{j\in[d]}\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{j}}\sigma^{2}_{S,j}|\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{j}}|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{S,j}-\sigma^{2}_{S,j}|\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{j\in S}\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{S,j}-\sigma^{2}_{S,j}|\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}|S|\right)\max_{j\in[d]}\max_{S\in\mathbb{S}_{j}}\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{S,j}-\sigma^{2}_{S,j}|\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\right\}.

Now, the standard Chernhoff method for subgaussian and subexponential random variables (see Propositions
27
and
30
in Appendix
D
) gives, for all
j\in[d]
, for all
S\in\mathbb{S}_{j}
, for all
x\leq 8\nu^{2}
(so that we can focus on the subgaussian regime),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}

\displaystyle\left\{|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}_{S,j}-\sigma^{2}_{S,j}|>x\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i\in[n_{S}]}(X_{S,ij}-\widehat{\mu}_{S,j})^{2}-\mathbb{E}[(X_{S,1j}-\mu_{S,j})^{2}]|>x\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{|n_{S}^{-1}\sum_{i\in[n_{S}]}(X_{S,ij}-\mu_{S,j})^{2}-\mathbb{E}[(X_{S,1j}-\mu_{S,j})^{2}]|>x/2\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{|\widehat{\mu}_{S,j}-\mu_{S,j}|^{2}>x/2\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left\{-\frac{n_{S}x^{2}}{1024\nu^{4}}\right\}+2\exp\left\{-\frac{n_{S}x}{8\nu^{2}}\right\}\leq 4\exp\left\{-\frac{n_{S}x}{1024\nu^{4}}\right\}\leq 4\exp\left\{-\frac{(\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}}n_{S})x^{2}}{1024\nu^{4}}\right\}.

This implies that, if
C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}\leq 16\nu^{2}
,
\mathbb{P}_{H_{0}}\left\{V(\widehat{\sigma}_{\mathbb{S}}^{2})\geq C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}/2\right\}\leq\alpha/2
is satisfied if

\left(\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}|S|\right)\exp\left\{-\frac{\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}}n_{S}C^{2}_{\alpha}}{1024\nu^{4}}\right\}\leq\frac{\alpha}{8},

which further shows that it is sufficient to take

C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}\geq K_{2}\nu^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log\left(\sum_{S\in\mathbb{S}}|S|/\alpha\right)}{\min_{S\in\mathbb{S}}n_{S}}},

(40)

for a universal constant
K_{2}>0
. In order to satisfy both (
39
) and (
40
) at the same time, it is sufficient to take the maximum between the two right-hand sides, while ensuring that
C_{\alpha}^{(R+V)}\leq\min\{2,16\nu^{2}\}
, and the first statement follows. As for the second part concerning the Type-II error, the proof follows similarly to that of the second part of Theorem
7
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10
.

For the
d
-cycle, our measure of consistency of the variances is

V(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})=1-\min_{j\in[d]}\min_{S\in\{\{j-1,j\},\{j,j+1\}\}}\sigma^{2}_{S,j}.

We will show that testing

H_{0}:V(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})=0\quad\text{ vs. }\quad H_{1}(\rho):V(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})>\rho,

requires at least a separation of the order
\sqrt{\log d/n}
, and since
H_{1}^{\prime}:R(\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}})+V(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbb{S}})>\rho
, the statement would follow. Formally, this corresponds to assuming that
\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}
is always compatible, and constructing prior distributions just on
\{V(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbb{S}})=0\}
and
\{V(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbb{S}})>\rho\}
. We construct a lower bound to show that the minimax separation in this case is at least
c_{1}\sqrt{\log d/\min_{i\in[d]}n_{i}}
, where
c_{1}>0
is a universal constant. Let
